SECTION 3OTHER CONSEQUENCES: DAMAGES AND ADMINISTRATIVE
                 SANCTIONS

ARTICLE 33 -- DAMAGES

33.1Liability of the granting authority

The granting authority cannot be held liable for any damage caused to the beneficiaries or to third
parties as a consequence of the implementation of the Agreement, including for gross negligence.

The granting authority cannot be held liable for any damage caused by any of the beneficiaries or
other participants involved in the action, as a consequence of the implementation of the Agreement.

33.2Liability of the beneficiaries

The beneficiaries must compensate the granting authority for any damage it sustains as a result of the
implementation of the action or because the action was not implemented in full compliance with the
Agreement, provided that it was caused by gross negligence or wilful act.

The liability does not extend to indirect or consequential losses or similar damage (such as loss of
profit, loss of revenue or loss of contracts), provided such damage was not caused by wilful act or
by a breach of confidentiality.

ARTICLE 34 -- ADMINISTRATIVE SANCTIONS AND OTHER MEASURES

Nothing in this Agreement may be construed as preventing the adoption of administrative sanctions
(i.e. exclusion from EU award procedures and/or financial penalties) or other public law measures,
in addition or as an alternative to the contractual measures provided under this Agreement (see,
for instance, Articles 135 to 145 EU Financial Regulation 2018/1046 and Articles 4 and 7 of
Regulation 2988/9522).

SECTION 4FORCE MAJEURE
ARTICLE 35 -- FORCE MAJEURE

22 Council Regulation (EC, Euratom) No 2988/95 of 18 December 1995 on the protection of the European Communities
  financial interests (OJ L 312, 23.12.1995, p. 1).

                                                                                                                                                              65

A party prevented by force majeure from fulfilling its obligations under the Agreement cannot be
considered in breach of them.

`Force majeure' means any situation or event that:

   - prevents either party from fulfilling their obligations under the Agreement,

   - was unforeseeable, exceptional situation and beyond the parties' control,

   - was not due to error or negligence on their part (or on the part of other participants involved
        in the action), and

   - proves to be inevitable in spite of exercising all due diligence.

Any situation constituting force majeure must be formally notified to the other party without delay,
stating the nature, likely duration and foreseeable effects.

The parties must immediately take all the necessary steps to limit any damage due to force majeure
and do their best to resume implementation of the action as soon as possible.

CHAPTER 6FINAL PROVISIONS

ARTICLE 36 -- COMMUNICATION BETWEEN THE PARTIES

36.1Forms and means of communication -- Electronic management

EU grants are managed fully electronically through the EU Funding & Tenders Portal (`Portal').

All communications must be made electronically through the Portal, in accordance with the Portal
Terms and Conditions and using the forms and templates provided there (except if explicitly instructed
otherwise by the granting authority).

Communications must be made in writing and clearly identify the grant agreement (project number
and acronym).

Communications must be made by persons authorised according to the Portal Terms and Conditions.
For naming the authorised persons, each beneficiary must have designated -- before the signature of
this Agreement -- a `legal entity appointed representative (LEAR)'. The role and tasks of the LEAR
are stipulated in their appointment letter (see Portal Terms and Conditions).

If the electronic exchange system is temporarily unavailable, instructions will be given on the Portal.

36.2Date of communication

The sending date for communications made through the Portal will be the date and time of sending,
as indicated by the time logs.

The receiving date for communications made through the Portal will be the date and time the
communication is accessed, as indicated by the time logs. Formal notifications that have not been
accessed within 10 days after sending, will be considered to have been accessed (see Portal Terms
and Conditions).

                                                                                                                                                              66
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with documHenEt RMeGf.AAr--es(M20u2lt4i)&47M25o8n9o8:-v011./007/2024

If a communication is exceptionally made on paper (by e-mail or postal service), general principles
apply (i.e. date of sending/receipt). Formal notifications by registered post with proof of delivery will
be considered to have been received either on the delivery date registered by the postal service or the
deadline for collection at the post office.

If the electronic exchange system is temporarily unavailable, the sending party cannot be considered
in breach of its obligation to send a communication within a specified deadline.

36.3Addresses for communication

The Portal can be accessed via the Europa website.

The address for paper communications to the granting authority (if exceptionally allowed) is the
official mailing address indicated on its website.

For beneficiaries, it is the legal address specified in the Portal Participant Register.

ARTICLE 37 -- INTERPRETATION OF THE AGREEMENT

The provisions in the Data Sheet take precedence over the rest of the Terms and Conditions of the
Agreement.

Annex 5 takes precedence over the Terms and Conditions; the Terms and Conditions take precedence
over the Annexes other than Annex 5.

Annex 2 takes precedence over Annex 1.

ARTICLE 38 -- CALCULATION OF PERIODS AND DEADLINES
In accordance with Regulation No 1182/7123, periods expressed in days, months or years are calculated
from the moment the triggering event occurs.

The day during which that event occurs is not considered as falling within the period.

`Days' means calendar days, not working days.

ARTICLE 39 -- AMENDMENTS

39.1Conditions
The Agreement may be amended, unless the amendment entails changes to the Agreement which
would call into question the decision awarding the grant or breach the principle of equal treatment
of applicants.

Amendments may be requested by any of the parties.

39.2Procedure

23 Regulation (EEC, Euratom) No 1182/71 of the Council of 3 June 1971 determining the rules applicable to periods, dates
  and time-limits (OJ L 124, 8/6/1971, p. 1).

The party requesting an amendment must submit a request for amendment signed directly in the Portal
Amendment tool.

The coordinator submits and receives requests for amendment on behalf of the beneficiaries (see
Annex 3). If a change of coordinator is requested without its agreement, the submission must be done
by another beneficiary (acting on behalf of the other beneficiaries).

The request for amendment must include:

   - the reasons why

   - the appropriate supporting documents and

   - for a change of coordinator without its agreement: the opinion of the coordinator (or proof that
        this opinion has been requested in writing).

The granting authority may request additional information.

If the party receiving the request agrees, it must sign the amendment in the tool within 45 days of
receiving notification (or any additional information the granting authority has requested). If it does
not agree, it must formally notify its disagreement within the same deadline. The deadline may be
extended, if necessary for the assessment of the request. If no notification is received within the
deadline, the request is considered to have been rejected.

An amendment enters into force on the day of the signature of the receiving party.

An amendment takes effect on the date of entry into force or other date specified in the amendment.

ARTICLE 40 -- ACCESSION AND ADDITION OF NEW BENEFICIARIES

40.1Accession of the beneficiaries mentioned in the Preamble

The beneficiaries which are not coordinator must accede to the grant by signing the accession form
(see Annex 3) directly in the Portal Grant Preparation tool, within 30 days after the entry into force
of the Agreement (see Article 44).

They will assume the rights and obligations under the Agreement with effect from the date of its entry
into force (see Article 44).

If a beneficiary does not accede to the grant within the above deadline, the coordinator must -- within
30 days -- request an amendment (see Article 39) to terminate the beneficiary and make any changes
necessary to ensure proper implementation of the action. This does not affect the granting authority's
right to terminate the grant (see Article 32).

40.2Addition of new beneficiaries

In justified cases, the beneficiaries may request the addition of a new beneficiary.

For this purpose, the coordinator must submit a request for amendment in accordance with Article 39.
It must include an accession form (see Annex 3) signed by the new beneficiary directly in the Portal
Amendment tool.

New beneficiaries will assume the rights and obligations under the Agreement with effect from the
date of their accession specified in the accession form (see Annex 3).

Additions are also possible in mono-beneficiary grants.

ARTICLE 41 -- TRANSFER OF THE AGREEMENT

In justified cases, the beneficiary of a mono-beneficiary grant may request the transfer of the grant to
a new beneficiary, provided that this would not call into question the decision awarding the grant or
breach the principle of equal treatment of applicants.

The beneficiary must submit a request for amendment (see Article 39), with

   - the reasons why

   - the accession form (see Annex 3) signed by the new beneficiary directly in the Portal
        Amendment tool and

   - additional supporting documents (if required by the granting authority).

The new beneficiary will assume the rights and obligations under the Agreement with effect from the
date of accession specified in the accession form (see Annex 3).

ARTICLE 42 -- ASSIGNMENTS OF CLAIMS FOR PAYMENT AGAINST THE
                     GRANTING AUTHORITY

The beneficiaries may not assign any of their claims for payment against the granting authority to
any third party, except if expressly approved in writing by the granting authority on the basis of a
reasoned, written request by the coordinator (on behalf of the beneficiary concerned).

If the granting authority has not accepted the assignment or if the terms of it are not observed, the
assignment will have no effect on it.

In no circumstances will an assignment release the beneficiaries from their obligations towards the
granting authority.

ARTICLE 43 -- APPLICABLE LAW AND SETTLEMENT OF DISPUTES

43.1Applicable law

The Agreement is governed by the applicable EU law, supplemented if necessary by the law of
Belgium.

Special rules may apply for beneficiaries which are international organisations (if any; see Data Sheet,
Point 5).

43.2Dispute settlement

If a dispute concerns the interpretation, application or validity of the Agreement, the parties must bring
action before the EU General Court -- or, on appeal, the EU Court of Justice -- under Article 272
of the Treaty on the Functioning of the EU (TFEU).

For non-EU beneficiaries (if any), such disputes must be brought before the courts of Brussels,
Belgium -- unless an international agreement provides for the enforceability of EU court judgements.

For beneficiaries with arbitration as special dispute settlement forum (if any; see Data Sheet, Point 5),
the dispute will -- in the absence of an amicable settlement -- be settled in accordance with the Rules
for Arbitration published on the Portal.

If a dispute concerns administrative sanctions, offsetting or an enforceable decision under Article 299
TFEU (see Articles 22 and 34), the beneficiaries must bring action before the General Court -- or, on
appeal, the Court of Justice -- under Article 263 TFEU.

For grants where the granting authority is an EU executive agency (see Preamble), actions against
offsetting and enforceable decisions must be brought against the European Commission (not against
the granting authority; see also Article 22).

ARTICLE 44 -- ENTRY INTO FORCE

The Agreement will enter into force on the day of signature by the granting authority or the
coordinator, depending on which is later.

PROJECT SUMMARY

Project summary

Grant Preparation (General Information screen) -- Provide an overall description of your project (including context and overall
objectives, planned activities and main achievements, and expected results and impacts (on target groups, change procedures,
capacities, innovation etc)). This summary should give readers a clear idea of what your project is about.

Use the project summary from your proposal.

Floating PV, if it is to aid the transition to a climate-neutral and resilient society and contribute towards the EU policy
goals, must overcome 3 challenges that are also high-lighted in the Work Programme. FPV must prove its sustainability,
by demonstrating low impact on biodiversity and satisfy end-of-life requirements, its longevity and reliability by
demonstrating system components that satisfy structural and functional requirements for the entire lifecycle, and its
affordability, by reducing the LCOE from FPV power plants.

These are the challenges that the objectives of SuRE seek to overcome. Activities are structured into 3 generalizable
topics, SUstainability, Reliability, and Efficiency, which gives SuRE FPV its name, and are designed to advance the entire
FPV industry. We will further work with concrete technology developments for 3 leading European FPV technologies
to improve their design, sustainability, cost-competitiveness and application range. The three FPV technology providers
are Ciel et Terre (CTI), who have installed 650 MW globally, Zimmermann PV-Steel Group (ZIM), who is dominating
the European FPV market, and Sunlit Sea (Sunlit) who is providing a innovative FPV solution for off-shore deployment.
CTI has recently prototyped a new floater design, which will be developed and tested in SuRE, first 50 kW, then on 5
MW scale. ZIM aims to expand their technology to higher sea states, and will build a 5 MW based on the developments
in floater-, connection- and anchoring- technology in SuRE. Sunlit are about to scale up their FPV technology and see
potential for large reductions in cost and CO2 footprint through the activities planned in SuRE. They will build a smaller,
but still commercially relevant, pilot of 100 on the Norwegian cost.

Ultimately, SuRE will provide both cost-efficient and sustainable new FPV technologies and generalizable knowledge,
thereby expanding the potential application areas without environmental sacrifices.

Work package WP1 ­ Sustainable FPV Power Plants

Work Package Number WP1                               Lead Beneficiary  8 - Fraunhofer

Work Package Name  Sustainable FPV Power Plants

Start Month              1 End Month                                                                         36

Objectives

O1.1) Determine the interaction of FPV with ecosystem biodiversity
O1.2) Develop methodologies and guidelines to assess the effects of FPV on aquatic systems and biodiversity
O1.3) Consider potential mitigation measures to counter negative impacts of FPV
O1.4) Methods development for impact quantification by enhancing existing tools and monitoring strategies
O1.5) Publish guidelines for sustainable FPV systems, including mitigating environmental impact.

Description

Task 1.1: Water body classification in Europe (M1-M36). Lead: Deltares. Participants: Deltares, IFE, LKY, BayWa,
ZIM, Sunlit

Commercial partners and Deltares will combine existing catalogue of FPV-amenable European waterbodies. Then, select
waters using European WFD classification and describe those suitable for FPV according to waterbody types (e.g.,
shallow lakes, deep reservoirs, intertidal zones), protected areas based on WFD and N2000, the existing ecosystem
services (e.g., drinking water, recreation, nature, water supply). The result is a decision support tool for waterbody
selection used in Task 1.2 & 1.3 and initial input to guidelines in Task 1.8. Fraunhofer will develop a monitoring concept
for FPV-water interaction. This includes monitoring hydrological variables such as oxygen levels and water temperatures,
air temperatures, and wind speed. Deltares develops a monitoring concept for FPV-aquatic food web interaction. Both
monitoring concepts are finalized in consultation with IFE, ZIM, LKY, and Sunlit. Commercial partners then integrate
sensors based on finalized monitoring concepts into FPV demonstrators in WP 4-5. Results are the monitoring protocol,
early data are for calibration and validation of modelling FPV-water interaction in Task 1.3, and 3-year dataset for Tasks
1.3-1.5.

Task 1.2: Modelling FPV impact on ecosystem (M1-M30). Lead Fraunhofer. Participants: Deltares, IFE

We determine maximum FPV coverage (using ratio of blocked irradiation, mRBI) for a wide range of typical European
waterbodies. At less than mRBI, environmental impact conforms to Water Framework Directive 2000/60/EC water
quality and biodiversity criteria. In addition, waterbody evaporation reduction will be computed as a function of
RBI. First, Fraunhofer, Deltares and IFE will calibrate their models with existing data from the demonstrator sites or
comparable sites elsewhere. Fraunhofer will run exploratory simulations in their GLM tool to determine approximate
mRBIs and evaporation reductions. Deltares will run detailed Delft3D simulations guided by GLM-derived mRBIs to
compute more refined values and with several FPV designs. Together with IFE, evaporation reductions are computed,
and results are compared with GLM 1D analysis to assess accuracy gains. SuRE partners calibrate and validate their
results with monitoring data from WPs 4-6. Results are enhanced, validated tools to assess the direct impacts of FPV on
aquatic systems (GLM and Delft3D), catalogue of maximum permissible FPV coverage ratios (mRBIs) differentiated
by waterbody type and several standard FPV designs, and dependence of evaporation reduction on FPV coverage ratio
for typical European waterbodies.

Task 1.3 Life-cycle Assessment of FPV (M6-M36). Lead: TNO. Participants: Fraunhofer, TNO, Deltares, SABIC

TNO will provide a base model for LCA that quantifies CO2 footprints for current FPV designs using procedures EF
3.0 and/or ReCiPe. Fraunhofer and Deltares will propose additional LCA metrics that capture FPV impact on waterbody
and biodiversity. Fraunhofer and TNO carry out LCA analyses for a variety of standard FPV designs, including those
used in demonstrators in WP 4-6. The LCA analysis of FPV systems will be enhanced by a cradle-to-grave approach
and additional impact categories such as biodiversity. SABIC contributes its expertise in plastic components of FPV
installations. TNO and Fraunhofer link to IEA Photovoltaic Power System Program Task 12 and project(s) funded by
HORIZON-CL5-2022-D3-03-09 for latest PV recycling concepts. Results are enhanced LCA methodology for FPV,
assessment of ecological impact of updated FPV design throughout their life cycle.

Task 1.4 Floater-centered end-of-life and recycling options (M1-M30). Lead: SABIC. Participants: TNO
SABIC will lead the establishment of the most cost-effective, sustainable end-of-life, and recycling options for plastic

                                                                                                             7
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

components in FPV designs used in SuRE. The goal is to improve recyclability of plastic floaters and other components
used in WP 4-6. Fraunhofer and TNO will use existing libraries for remaining FPV components, including PV modules,
cables, connectors and inverters. Results are financially feasible and sustainable end-of-life strategies for major FPV
designs.

Task 1.5 Methodology, mitigation measures and guidelines to assess environmental impact of FPV(M1-36) Lead:
Deltares, Participants: All

Deltares conducts scenario studies of high-yield, low-impact FPV, preferably for demonstrator sites in WP 4-6 and
option to include similar sites elsewhere. Studies include sessions with stakeholders, technicians, landscape designers,
and ecologists to evaluate perception of different designs in the context in the three pillars of sustainability: Residents,
direct users of the water body (e.g., recreation), and public at large, governments, policy makers; Producers and energy
companies; Environmental targets (WFD N2000) and nature conservationists. Results are definitions of barriers and
enablers for FPV with a positive impact, and pathways to implementing nature inclusive FPV designs. Based on
results Tasks 1.1-1.5, Deltares and Fraunhofer will propose adjustments to FPV to increase sustainability. Numerical
simulations of a) methods to increase light penetration without compromise on PV yield, b) plant location evaluation
within waterbody to foster fish stock, and c) added structures specifically for aquatic life. Consequences of proposed
design changes for durability, LCOE, and electric yields will be numerically evaluated in WPs 2 & 3 and refined
with commercial partners. Results are a list of options for project demonstrators to enhance sustainability and for
generalizability in pre-normative standards. Finally, Fraunhofer and Deltares will compile guidelines for development
and implementation of FPV solutions with a minimally negative and/or maximally positive environmental impact. The
guidelines incorporate and summarize the learnings and results of all WPs. Results are published guidelines for design
and implementation of sustainable FPV solutions.

Work package WP2 ­ Reliable FPV Power plants

Work Package Number WP2                               Lead Beneficiary  7 - TNO

Work Package Name  Reliable FPV Power plants

Start Month                                   1 End Month                                                       36

Objectives

O2.1) Perform systematic large-scale study on field failures and stress profiles from portfolio of FPV assets
O2.2) Develop simulation models to support understanding of degradation root causes and help system design
O2.3) Develop and publish recommended accelerated reliability test procedures for components, and use these to
evaluate mitigation strategies for component failures

Description

Task 2.1: Large scale study on field degradation (M1-18). Lead: TNO. Participants: IFE, Fraunhofer, LKY, CTI, BayWa,
ZIM, Sunlit

Statistics on degradation modes will be collected from within and outside the consortium. The consortium has access
to a broad collection of system designs, deployment conditions, and associated degradation modes for both inland and
near shore water bodies. Information on degradation modes will be collected from field observations and data analyses,
aerial thermography, combined with time-series analysis for degradation mode detection and power loss quantification
(when possible), visual inspection, and field IV curve tracing, and site-sporadic bird soiling. Results are key degradation
modes and statistics, used as input for definition of accelerated lifetime test procedures and mitigation of degradation
modes for Tasks 2.3-5.

Task 2.2 Quantifying field stressors (M3-36). Lead: IFE. Participants: All

Gathering field data on FPV-specific stress profiles by instrumenting sites with at least two different floater technologies
with specific sensors to assess mechanical loads (using strain gauges and accelerometers), salt exposure (for the near-
shore conditions), humidity, and component time of wetness. Focus areas are mechanical loads on hinges, cables,
inverters, and PV modules; salt exposure of modules, junction boxes and connectors; local humidity levels at inverters
and underneath modules for pontoon-based structures; and time-of-wetness at junction boxes, module edges and

                                                                                                                8
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

connectors. Results are FPV-specific stress profiles for each component to validate stress and degradation mode
modelling in Task 2.3, and to guide accelerated stress test development in Task 2.4

Task 2.3 Stress and degradation mode modelling (M3-36). Lead: IFE. Participants: Fraunhofer, CTI, ZIM, Sunlit

Develop multiscale modelling chain for assessing mechanical load and wear on individual FPV components in relevant
design scenarios. We will carry out model developments for, and establish proper interfaces between, three state-of-
the-art modelling tools: (1) Aero-hydro-servo-elastic simulation software 3DFloat (IFE), made originally for floating
wind turbines using the FPV-relevant structural and hydrodynamic modules. (2) Computational fluid dynamics (CFD)
modelling software Ansys Fluent (IFE) / COMSOL (Fraunhofer) for wind loads. (3) Finite-element modelling (FEM)
software COMSOL (Fraunhofer) / SiSim (IFE) for detailed studies on PV modules. For 3DFloat, several modelling
adaptations and improvements will be assessed and implemented as necessary to accurately represent the FPV design
scenarios. For structure, we will test replacing finite element beam sections with basic shell elements to represent floating
components more accurately. For waves and wave-structure interactions, we will test the need for more advanced and
tailored wave spectra, as well as n-body problem-based potential theory modelling to capture neighbor interaction and
array effects. FEM will be used to study humidity ingress into PV modules and other electrical components, based
on humidity stress profiles gathered in Task 2.2. The results will be compared with results from accelerated stress
testing in Task 2.4 and GPV modelling to assess the need for extended or adapted humidity stress testing. FEM-based
thermal modelling (Fraunhofer) will assess hotspot formation from bird droppings, based on case studies selected from
sites monitored in the project. Results will be calculation of mechanical loads/stresses, humidity ingress and hotspot
susceptibility for the different designs scenarios. This will feed into the stress test development in Task 2.4, as well as
into the technology-specific WPs 4-6. From the loads/stresses both component ultimate limit states and fatigue limit
states will be established.

Task 2.4 Develop accelerated reliability test procedures and recommended FPV-specific standards (M7-M36) Lead:
Fraunhofer. Participants: All

Design and conduct meaningful laboratory tests to extend PV components testing to FPV challenges using degradation
mode statistics from Task 2.1, stress profiles from Task 2.2, and simulation results from Task 2.3. Target components
include PV modules, cables, connectors, and power electronics. We will focus on FPV-specific stressors: mechanical,
moisture/salt stress, and hotspots caused by isolated soiling such as bird droppings. Preliminary submersion tests towards
qualifying panels for FPV. Selected components will be subjected to test procedures of single stressors, combined, or
sequential stress tests. Results are the published test procedures and defined test criteria., input for Task 2.5. The results
from Tasks 2.1-2.5 will be summarized to create pre-normative test guidelines for FPV equipment. The result is published
state-of-art requirements and tests for FPV components, designed to foster co-development with component suppliers
thereby de-risking future FPV.

Task 2.5 Assess, design, and apply mitigation strategies for failure and degradation modes (M13-M36) Lead: TNO.
Participants: Fraunhofer, IFE, CTI, ZIM, BayWa, Sunlit, MetSolar

Improved mitigation strategies will be assessed for effectiveness in several steps. One, from degradation and stressor
findings of Tasks 2.1-3, we will determine one or more mitigation methods for modules and components and/or their
combinations. Two, by using accelerated ageing results from Task 2.4, test for failures by applying the accelerated testing
to components with and without mitigation strategies. Purpose-made samples will be manufactured by TNO. Results are
recommended improvements for material, components, and their combinations.

Work package WP3 ­ Efficient FPV Power plants

Work Package Number WP3                               Lead Beneficiary  1 - IFE

Work Package Name  Efficient FPV Power plants

Start Month              1 End Month                                                                  36

Objectives

O3.1) Increase bankability for new FPV systems through more accurate EYAs
O3.2) Set up a simulation framework that enable identification of potential design improvements
O3.3) Enable improved O&M of FPV power plants by combining the models developed into a digital twin.

                                                                                                      9
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

Description

Task 3.1: Monitoring, data collection and data quality (M1-M24) Lead: IFE. Participants: TNO, Fraunhofer, CTI, LKY,
BayWa, ZIM, Sunlit

The aim of this task is to ensure that the data-basis for carrying out the work inn all the WPs in the project is as good as it
possibly can be. Production and monitoring data from real sites is indispensable to validate models and extract reference
values. Monitoring- and production data from power plants, pilots, and test sites of different sizes, will be collected from
all partners, including production and monitoring data from 18 sites owned by CTI/LKY, 3 sites owned by BayWa, and
3 sites owned by Sunlit. Data from these sites will be quality controlled, and additional monitoring equipment will be
installed if needed. The research partners also have ownership of data from various smaller FPV systems that will also
be utilized in the project. This includes a well instrumented Ciel & Terre system in South Africa, TNOs test site in the
Netherlands and Fraunhofers's German test site.

Task 3.2: Quantification of performance losses for FPV (M3-M30) Lead: IFE. Participants: TNO, Fraunhofer, CTI, LKY,
BayWa, ZIM, Sunlit, Metsolar

To assess thermal loss, CFD models previously developed at IFE and Fraunhofer will a) be developed to include
evaporative cooling, and b) be using the specific geometries and material properties of the FPV technologies as
input. Together with production and monitoring data (Task 3.1) the models will be used to analyse efficiency of the
different cooling mechanisms (convective, radiative, conductive, evaporative), as a function of climatic parameters.
Recommended practice on how to include thermal losses of FPV power plants in the most widely used commercial tool,
PVsyst, will be published. To assess PLR and soiling loss rates, the work will follow two routes previously developed for
ground-based PV and published by IFE19 and Fraunhofer. One is based on an analytical, iterative approach ("Combined
Degradation and Soiling loss, CODS"), the other is based on machine learning on order to decompose performance index
time series into a soiling component, a degradation component, and a seasonal component. Further model development
will be to adapt filtering routines to account for FPV specific losses, such as WIML or local soiling variation or other
FPV specific signatures. To assess WIML, TNO and IFE will model mismatch and compared to monitoring values for
the technologies in the consortium at selected sites. Fixed POA is translated to moving POA irradiance given different
sea states and the movement of the respective FPV technology. The hydrodynamic models will be developed or adapted
in WP2. From this, an electrical model will be used to calculate the mismatch in each string, and the effect of different
inverters/maximum power point trackers is analysed. Finally, a wave-induce mismatch corrected Performance Ratio is
calculated.

Task 3.3: Digital twins (M12-M36) Lead: IFE. Participants: Fraunhofer, TNO, CTI, LKY, BayWa, ZIM, Sunlit, Metsolar

With the major loss mechanisms for the three FPV technologies identified, simulated, and quantified through work in
Tasks 3.1 and 3.2, the work in this task will combine them to make a digital twin to study interdependencies and potential
improvements in the design, and potentially to continuously benchmark the live performance of the power plants. The
work will be done with two levels of complexity. First, a digital twin of the PV power plant will be built in python.
This is based on modifications of existing code developed at IFE and Fraunhofer. The parameterization of thermal losses
(U-values) and WIML that has been developed in Task 3.2 will be used to reduce the processing time and interaction
between different types of models. Representative soiling loss rates and PLRs based on results from Task 3.2 will also
be used as input. Second, we will explore a more complex digital twin combining the actual simulations performed in
Task 3.2, this will be done for one FPV technology. This digital twin is expected to be computationally heavy, and we
will study and quantify the inaccuracies inferred by various simplifications applied to the individual simulations.

Task 3.4. Optimizing cell- and module technology for FPV applications (M6-M24) Lead: MetSolar. Participants: IFE,
TNO, Sunlit, BayWa, CTI, LKY

Production costs of different cell and module technologies versus the resulting efficiency in the field will be analysed.
Field efficiency will be calculated for the three FPV technologies in the consortium and applications in Europe. Then, we
will evaluate the cell- and module selections that contribute to the lowest LCOEs. Next, MetSolar will consider adaptions
in their production-lines to accommodate the optimization. The choice of adaptions must follow from the analysis, but
three likely pathways have been identified; 1) prismatic glass to adapt to low incident angles for FPV, 2) change in cell
size to M10/M12 to have higher efficiency modules to offset the greater BoS cost, and 3) adaptions to reduce PRL and
improve reliability for FPV applications, based on results from Task 2.5.

Task 3.5: LCOE calculations (M6-M36) Lead: TNO. Participants: Fraunhofer, CTI, LKY, BayWa, ZIM, Sunlit, Metsolar,
IFE.

This task will set up the methodology for LCOE calculations to be used as a foundation when proposing options for cost-

                                                      10
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

effective engineering solutions throughout the project and to enable comparative LCOE estimations. LCOE is strongly
dependent on location, type of water body and choice of technology, so a bottom-up approach28 will be used. To consider
the impact on LCOE for different use cases, a set of parameters such as sea state, locations, water depth, etc. will be
defined. For each parameter, the relation with the different components of LCOE calculations is defined. Correlation
between given parameters and energy yield and degradation is found in Task 3.2, whilst the respective impact on CAPEX
and OPEX is done together with the FPV technology providers within the consortium.

Work package WP4 ­ Development and piloting of new CTI FPV tech.

Work Package Number WP4                               Lead Beneficiary  2 - CTI

Work Package Name  Development and piloting of new CTI FPV tech.

Start Month              1 End Month                                                       36

Objectives

O4.1) Test and verify basic design of new CTI technology
O4.2) Reduce LCA further through assessing recycled materials with lower carbon footprint
O4.3) Optimize design for next generation of the technology

Description

Task 4.1: CTI Pilot of at least 5 MW (M1 to M18). Lead: CTI. Participants: LKY

First large-scale installation (5 MW pilot) of fundamentally new CTI FPV technology, with reduced LCOE and improved
LCA compared to the existing CTI FPV technology, Hydrelio, and installation follow-up (the installation itself will be
done by CTI's customer and subcontractors). The new technology used for the 5 MW pilot consists of radically new
and lighter floaters which reduce transportation costs significantly, and the new O&M concept, where O&M is carried
out without operators stepping on the modules. A pilot of 50 kW will be installed to assess the new system design for
a broader range of wave conditions. A standard SCADA and monitoring system will be installed by the customer while
IFE, TNO, Fraunhofer, and Deltares will contribute with installation of additional sensors packages for biodiversity,
performance, and reliability of structure and anchoring. Data from the pilot will be used to perform measurements and
verify models in WP1, 2, 3, and 4. The pilot forms an essential basis for further developments, as described in Tasks 4.2-8.

Task 4.2 Sustainable materials (M1 to M36) Lead: CTI. Participants: SABIC, TNO

Using ongoing learnings from pilot (Task 4.1), integrate and validate sustainable materials. Start with selecting materials
meeting criteria of sourcing, mechanical fitness, water-stable, toxicity, and sustainability. Testing performed for ageing,
mechanical strength, and compatibility with industrial production. Materials used in pilot (Task 4.1) will be compared
to more sustainable (such as recycled) materials in order to improve performance and carbon footprint. CTI will
collaborate with SABIC (WP1) and TNO for ageing tests (WP2).

Task 4.3 Assessment of reliability for different loads, environments, and sea states (M1 to M24) Lead: CTI. Participants:
TNO, IFE

Measurements of mechanical loads and strain on structures and mooring/anchoring of the 5 MW pilot will be used
to validate and adapt models developed prior to pilot installation. Use best-known accelerated stress tests from WP2
to perform CTI-specific ageing and fatigue tests. Complimentary mechanical tests (tensile tests, ageing, fatigue, and
corrosion tests) will be performed with CTI internal lab, and in collaboration with TNO and IFE in Tasks 2.2 and 2.3.
Results are quantified by showing a) reduction of over-design and b) subsequent LCOE reduction. A bench development
will allow to qualify CTI pilot's structure in relation to its interactions with the panel.

Task 4.4 Assessing and optimizing the next generation of the CTI design, including O&M optimizations and Anchoring
(M1 to M36). Lead: CTI. Participants: LKY, Fraunhofer, IFE, TNO, Deltares

Inputs from work on anchoring, O&M optimization, Tasks 4.1-4.3, WP2 (mechanical strength) and WP3 (losses, LCOE)
will be used to assess and optimize the new system to reach TRL6. Anchoring simulations with CTI internal model for
interaction between the floating structure and its anchoring system to understand mechanical loads passing through the
structure and the anchoring system. Considerations include wind pattern for FPV design to include gust size applied for

                                                                                           11
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

FPV anchoring design. In parallel, IFE will do the same with their own models. Comparisons among the models will
allow model improvement and optimizing the anchoring system design used on the pilot (Task 4.1) and go from TRL 4
to 6/7. The design modifications are planned to be installed on next generation CTI product. For efficient O&M, CTI has
developed a bespoke maintenance device which will be tested on the pilot. To date, an experimental proof of concept
exists, and target is to improve it for use in the operational environment of the pilot (from TRL3 to 7). Finally, CTI,
with input from SABIC, Fraunhofer, TNO, IFE, and MetSolar, will assess CAPEX, OPEX, LCOE and LCA of the 5
MW plant as a benchmark, and then co-optimize the individual considerations and improvements studied in Task 4.1-5
against each other. This includes everything from the ease of the industrial production processes of the chosen design to
logistics, mechanical strength and expected lifetime, O&M, and expected production.

Task 4.5 Water quality and biodiversity impact (M1 to M36) Lead: CTI. Participants: LKY, Deltares, Fraunhofer

To assess water quality and toxicity, CTI/LKY will monitor changes in water at different water strata and assess its
chemical composition. CTI, Fraunhofer, and Deltares will use sampling methodology developed in WP1, including
pre-, mid-, and post-install of the pilot (Task 4.1). CTI and LKY will also contribute with data from existing FPV
plants in Japan. To assess impact on biodiversity, Deltares will pre-assess the pilot using ecological inventories based
acoustic recording, visit of experts and DNA samples. Inventory will cover the species on the site (avifauna, pisciculture,
entomofauna, flora) to have an exhaustive analysis. A sizing of biodiversity enhancement measures will also be carried
out, as examples, for installation of ponds for amphibians and walls for swallows. For monitoring, Deltares will visit the
site pre-, mid- and post-install to measure the FPV plant and enhancement-measures impacts. Data sharing with WP1
to include in generalizable results, modelling, and guidelines.

Task 4.6 Business case. Lead: CTI. Participants: LKY (M1 ­ M30)

CTI and LKY will study the business environment related to the project outcomes. The task will start from the beginning
of the project and will be closely aligned with research and pilot activities. Objectives will be a) to explore the marketing
opportunities, b) to analyze the drivers and barriers for the adoption of the technology among stakeholders of the
whole value chain, c) to define new business models, and d) share the achievements (project realizations) in the proper
communication channels to show and expand the potential market. Business interest, market attractiveness and business
models will be evaluated. The task will feed the Consortium exploitation plan (Task 7.2), while sensitive outcomes will
remain internal to LKY and CTI.

Work package WP5 ­ Development and piloting of Zimmermann FPV technology

Work Package Number WP5                               Lead Beneficiary    3 - ZIM

Work Package Name  Development and piloting of Zimmermann FPV technology

Start Month              1 End Month                                               36

Objectives

O5.1) Concept development ZIM Float for rougher waters & environment to increase standard design principles to SSC4
(1.5-2 m significant wave hight)
O5.2) Reduce CAPEX and OPEX by adequate design and selection of BOS components and materials
O5.3) Build 5 MW pilot that combines WP1-3 best practices with ZIM Float and BayWa system design

Description

Task 5.1: Component updates, lab and field testing under simulated conditions (Hardware) (M1-M18). Lead: ZIM.
Participants: BayWa, GRL, TNO, IFE

Modify component designs for use on flexible island with SSCs 2-4. Inputs are conditions identified in Tasks 5.2-3.
Use computational and prototype tests of new materials and/or concepts to test structural pieces that link island parts.
Tests include force, vibration, corrosion, cyclic cable bending, inverter and transformer shaking and swinging, bending
construction and connectors, material interaction, bio-interaction and biofouling, and system-mooring connectors.
Testing conditions are humidity, salinity, cyclic loading based on results of Task 5.2. Results are top candidates to be
tested /implemented in Tasks 5.3-4.

Task 5.2: Measure island motion, forces, and environment (M1-M18) Lead: BayWa. Participants: ZIM, IFE, TNO, GRL

                                                                                   12
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

ZIM and BayWa will identify existing plants for installing wave, motion, force, and ambient sensors. Sensor type,
deployment method, and analysis will be developed with IFE and TNO. Inputs are from WP2. Targets are quantifying
environmental stressors, load cycles, and deriving how degradation factors interact. Candidate factors are global island
movement, humidity, salinity, and sunlight; candidate failures are in inverters, transformer stations, modules, and
connectors. Results are analysis of data which will are input to Tasks 5.2-4.
Task 5.3: From met ocean data to design standards (M6-M30) Lead: BayWa. Participants: ZIM, IFE, TNO, GRL

Use literature review and measurements from Task 5.2 to connect water body categories with environmental conditions
and FPV design. Assemble optimized components in Task 5.1, measurements in Task 5.2, and WP1-3 results to make
decision tree for faster, standardized FPV design. Incorporate optimized FPV island flexibility to reduce material use,
decreases lifetime costs, and adhere to sustainability procedures. Results are the candidates to be tested in pilot Task 5.4
and information feedback/feedforward with Tasks 5.2-3 and WP1-3.

Task 5.4: ZIM/BayWa Pilot of at least 5 MW and subsequent analysis (M18-M36). Leads: BayWa. Participants: ZIM,
IFE, Deltares, Fraunhofer, TNO, GRL

Build 5 MW pilot using up-to-date advances from SuRE. Incorporating best practices of own technology development
from Tasks 5.1-3 and WPs 1-3. Pilot contains extra sensors for added research benefit and accelerated expansion to higher
SSCs, choice of sensors defined in WP1-3. Results are customer-connected pilot with future access for measurement
and research. The first annual cycle will be used to assess improvements and verify models and methodology developed
in SuRE.

Task 5.5: Pre-, mid-, and post-Pilot install environmental studies (M1-M36). Lead: Deltares. Participants: BayWa, ZIM,
Fraunhofer

Use pilot site to implement environmental impact tests on a specific FPV project to show general applicability. Use
methodologies of Task 1.6 and early draft guidelines of Task 1.8 to establish protocols and measurements at pilot site
before, during, and after installation of the pilot from Task 5.4. Results are timeline study, recommended improvements
to guidelines of Task 1.8, and recommended improvements to future FPV.

Work package WP6 ­ Development of Sunlit's integrated FPV technology

Work Package Number WP6                               Lead Beneficiary              5 - Sunlit

Work Package Name  Development of Sunlit's integrated FPV technology

Start Month              1 End Month                                                            36

Objectives

O6.1) Developing a digital product development cycle for Sunlit's aluminum floater
O6.2) Optimized selection of new floater generation prototype

Description

Task 6.1: FEM model development for pressing of aluminum (M1-M24). Lead: IFE. Participants: Sunlit

Model development specifically for WP6 will be on aluminum-float pressing, central to Sunlit manufacturing. IFE will
extend its existing FEM modelling ecosystem to handle numerically challenging large metal deformations that occur
in this process. The new tool, FormSim, will be developed as a module for existing tools SiSim and AlSim, where
additional algorithms will be implemented to handle large mesh deformations and for anisotropic yield stress model.
Further, code developments will implement higher order plasticity elements, robust triggers, thresholds for remeshing,
and error bounded remeshing. Results are used in Tasks 6.2 (and further in 6.3-4)

Task 6.2 Model harmonization and interfacing for holistic digital product development (M12 ­ M26). Lead: IFE.
Participants: Sunlit, TNO

Digital product development cycle implementation, using all key design parameters of the aluminum float. Sunlit guide
establishing and implementing interfaces between the following modelling tools: i) Combined FEM and CFD model
chain to get mechanical stress profiles on the float for a given design case, developed in WT2.3 (IFE). ii) CFD modelling

                                                                                                13
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

of fluid and heat flows, developed in WT 3.2 (IFE). iii) FEM model of the aluminum pressing, developed in WT6.1
(IFE). iv) LCA model developed in WT1.3 (TNO). Especially important is harmonizing input and output data from the
different models to enable automated mapping of the parameter space and rapid iteration turnover. Results are modelling
capabilities at different accuracy-levels will be used actively to quickly screen the parameter space and converge to
promising design candidates. Results are used in Task 6.3 (and further in 6.4)

Task 6.3 Pareto frontier mapping & prototype candidate selection (M20 ­ M32). Lead: Sunlit. Participants: IFE,
Fraunhofer, Metsolar

Use modelling framework from Task 6.2 to efficiently map Pareto frontier of float-design parameter space. Key design
parameters for exploration because of co-impact on sustainability, reliability, and cost are (1) Reduced aluminum
thickness and/or aluminum feedstock quality, such as with recycled aluminum; 2) Modified floater shape for increased
mechanical robustness in rougher waters and/or for lower aluminum consumption; (3) Changed floater size to reduce
unit costs (from cabling, factory operations, PV cell and module, etc.) per square meter; (4) Improved cup design to
achieve more homogeneous water cooling for improved performance and reliability. This work will happen in concert
with Metsolar, who will harness the modularity of the PV industry and match module to float design. Further, Sunlit's
bill of materials compendium will help identify more- and less-expensive alternatives to today's production. Enhancing
the hinge strength is critical for the durability and reliability of Sunlit Sea's FPV systems. This is thoroughly addressed in
SuRE, in collaboration with SABIC. The other central development to withstand harsher conditions, is and a shift from
butyl/silicon encapsulants to polyolefin elastomer (POE). This shift will leverage MetSolar's cutting-edge capabilities in
lamination techniques by transitioning from the current potting process to advanced lamination techniques. MetSolar's
extensive experience in metal lamination will guide our exploration of using polyolefin elastomer (POE) instead of
traditional butyl/silicon encapsulants. New, market-available optical components, PV cells and prismatic glass, will be
examined to further push these fronts. Results are prototype designs from Pareto analysis, used in Task 6.4.

Task 6.4 Prototype production and field deployment (M28 ­ M36) Lead: Sunlit. Participants: IFE, Metsolar

Informed by models and design process developed and carried out in Tasks 6.1-6.3, prototypes will be built. Protype
testing in subcontracted wave-tank with extra sensors will validate simulated results. Sunlit will manufacture small press
tools using existing machinery built to test the most critical parts of stretch forming. The best design will be the basis
for the next generation press tool. The press tool will be built and installed in a large hydraulic press to make full-
size product. Financing for the tool is outside of SuRE, the Innovation Fund will be considered. Final wave-tank tests
will verify the design. Sunlit will build an (approximately) 100 kW pilot installation by the end SuRE (Singløya Nord,
Norway) with guarantees of future access to production data SuRE research partners.

Work package WP7 ­ Dissemination & Communication

Work Package Number  WP7                              Lead Beneficiary  11 - COMPAZ
Work Package Name                                                                                      36
Start Month          Dissemination & Communication

                          1 End Month

Objectives

O7.1) Targeted strategic communication that strengthen and expand synergies
O7.2) Timely and appropriate dissemination of SuRE outcomes to appropriate Target Groups
O7.3) Coordinate innovation efforts and use consortium strengths to maximize exploitation of KERs

Description

Task 7.1 Develop and implement communication and dissemination plan (CDP) (M1-M36). Lead: Compáz. Participants:
All

The task will be focused on the definition, coordination, and implementation of the CDP. In the first six months, the
SuRE identity (logo, graphical chart) and the Kit of Communication Materials (website, brochure, template, flyers,
video) will be developed. The first CDP, D7.1, will be released at M6, with subsequent updated plans in M18 and M30.
Social network accounts for the project will be set up. From M6 and onwards, the framework developed, will be filled
with content and regularly updated to inform about project news. The partners will attend the targeted conferences,
publications, lectures, outreach events and workshops as detailed in Sec 2.2.1. Compaz will perform Communication

                                                                                                   14
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

activities as detailed in Section 2.2.3 and track the Dissemination activities. All partners have planned activities to
contribute to communication and dissemination of SuRE results. In addition to Compaz and IFE, BayWa, with their
strong network among water-body owners, drinking water utilities, hydropower operators and public authorities, will
have a particularly active role within the dissemination work. Contribute, upon invitation by the CINEA, to common
information and dissemination activities to increase the visibility and synergies between Horizon Europe supported
actions.

Task 7.2 Exploitation and Innovation management (M1-M36). Lead: IFE. Participants: All

IFE implements innovation management to allow SuRE to respond to external and internal opportunities to exploit
creative efforts to introduce new ideas, services, processes, and products. Key elements have been listed in Sec 2.2.3. Key
SuRE innovation management is monitoring innovation actions and supporting partners to enhance their own innovation
capacity to integrate new knowledge. Task 7.2 identifies companies' go-to-market needs and to provide support to
enhance their own potential for impact delivery. Innovation management will be closely related to the project tasks on
exploitation and defining the key exploitable results. The consortium partners will have individual exploitation plans
feeding into the Consortium exploitation plan delivered in M12. Innovation management will be discussed in every
General Assembly, with concessions for limited-audience conversation when required.

Task 7.3 FPV SuRE short films (M1-M36). Lead: Compáz. Participants: All

Compáz will create yearly 2-min short films about the project goals and the global objectives and vision of the project.
The films will be produced and released from the start of the project each year. The content of these videos will be the
communication line and content of the communication activities and diffused on the social media. It will serve SuRE in
its communication activities to reach larger audiences beyond FPV professionals.

Work package WP8 ­ Coordination & Management

Work Package Number WP8                               Lead Beneficiary  1 - IFE

Work Package Name  Coordination & Management

Start Month              1 End Month                                                                            36

Objectives

O8.1) Coordinate SuRE; oversee R&I and ensure communication between partners
O8.2) Project management: Quality assurance, time planning, reporting (according to CA), and financial control
O8.3) Anticipation and evasion of risks, problems, and conflicts
O8.4) Definition, implementation, and compliance of data management plan
O8.5) Elevate novelties for fair allocation of IPR according to Consortium Agreement

Description

Task 8.1 Admin, Financial, Legal Management, and European Commission Reports (M1-M36). Lead: IFE

Project management is via consolidated management of the different processes (costs, resources, communication, content
and delay, risks, purchase, legal, and excellent). PM includes managing interactions among partners, WPs, activities,
external relationships, and managing modification and deviations. Task 8.1 includes management of overall SuRE
progress through meetings and technical and financial reporting. Reports will be collected from WP leaders on 6-month
intervals. WP8 will manage preparations and dissemination of reports. IFE, as coordinator, will ensure communication
and reporting with EC, including any potential major issues or changes in the work plan. IFE will oversee establishment
and monitoring of governing boards.

Task 8.2 Risk management (M1-M36). Lead: IFE

Risk management ensures proper risk handling and prediction, impacts estimation, and mitigation execution. Each
participant, from partners to the General Assembly (GA), is responsible for continuous potential risk monitoring and
reporting to the coordinator to allow early identification and mitigation. Identified risks will be brought to the GA for
amelioration.

                                                                                                                15
Project: 101135567 -- SuRE -- HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01

                                                      Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

Task 8.3 Research data: Management plan and standardization (M1-M36). Lead: IFE

SuRE is composed of a broad group of researchers, commercial actors, and communication specialists. We will produce
large and heterogenous datasets, some of which will be proprietary, others obfuscated to protect its origins, and others
for the public domain. Further, we will also generate several statistical and mechanistic models and the results of their
implementation. Task 8.3 will develop and implement the SuRE data management plan according to FAIR principles;
current recommendations or standards provide guidelines for big and small datasets, codes and models. IFE will generate
and distribute metadata templates supporting FAIR handling and completion using the public resource Norwegian Centre
for Research Data (NSD). Common procedures and standard methodologies will follow international guidelines for
maximal value and comparability. The Management Board (MB) will agree and circulate written guidelines and standard
operating procedures for implementation. Task 8.3 provides continuous FAIR documentation and data handling support
to all WPs.

Task 8.4 Sex and gender dimensions in FPV (M1-M36) Lead: IFE. Participants: All

Kickoff, mid-point, and closure meetings include a workshop session with external advisor who will address innovation
and workplace aspects leading to improved gender equality at all levels of PV industry.

Deliverable D1.1 ­ Monitoring Concepts

Deliverable Number  D1.1                      Lead Beneficiary             9 - Deltares
Deliverable Name    Monitoring Concepts
Type                OTHER                     Dissemination Level          PU - Public
Due Date (month)                           6 Work Package No               WP1

Description

Establishing monitoring protocol for measuring hydrological and ambient variables (e.g., oxygen levels, water
temperatures, air temperatures, wind speed). This will also entail guidelines for the selection of suitable waterbodies
and help to validate models now and in the future beyond this project. The SuRE monitoring protocol aims at giving
guidance to permitting bodies and commercial parties.

Deliverable D1.2 ­ FPV coverage

Deliverable Number  D1.2                              Lead Beneficiary     8 - Fraunhofer
Deliverable Name
Type                FPV coverage                                           PU - Public
Due Date (month)                                                           WP1
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                           30 Work Package No

Description

A scientific paper showing different scenario's and their environmental impact. Scenarios being combinations of
coverage ratios on various waterbodies. The scenarios are realistic and economical feasible. Modelling results show the
impact on water quality and food web of each scenario. Results will show the level of dependency of plant-design and
water body characteristics in relation to the expected environmental impact.

Deliverable D1.3 ­ LCA report

Deliverable Number  D1.3                              Lead Beneficiary     7 - TNO
Deliverable Name
Type                LCA report                                             PU - Public
Due Date (month)                                                           WP1
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                           24 Work Package No

Description

An enhanced LCA methodology for FPV and an assessment of ecological impact of updated FPV designs as investigated
in WP4-6. The assessment will include carbon footprint and additional impact categories such as biodiversity

Deliverable Name

Due Date (month)                                                           WP1
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                           24 Work Package No

Report on cost-effective and sustainable end-of-life and recycling options for plastic components used in SuRE FPV
designs. CTI floats and Sunlit Sea hinges identified as the most likely components to be studied.

Deliverable Name
Type                Biodiversity mitigation                                SEN - Sensitive
Due Date (month)                                                           WP1
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                             30 Work Package No

Description

Review of FPV designs for biodiversity and potential pathways to nature inclusive FPV designs. Based on the model-
runs (del 1.2) and the sessions with stakeholders we propose adjustments to FPV to increase sustainability. Results
of the deliverables 1.1 to 1.4 should be incorporated. Result is a list of possible options ­ best practices - to increase
sustainability.

Deliverable D5.1 ­ Load model

Deliverable Name
Type                Load model                                             SEN - Sensitive
Due Date (month)                                                           WP5
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                           12 Work Package No

Description

Accurate Hydrodynamic model of loads on connectors & steel structure

A hydrodynamic model that calculates the forces on connectors and the steel structure of floating photovoltaic (FPV)
systems. The model provides a detailed understanding of how forces from wind, waves, and currents interact with the
FPV system to enable the design of more reliable and durable FPV structures, which can withstand harsher environments
(higher SSC state levels), while avoiding overengineering.

Deliverable Name
Type                Design specifications pilot                            SEN - Sensitive
Due Date (month)                                                           WP5
                    R -- Document, report             Dissemination Level

                                           18 Work Package No

Description

The 'Design Specifications for Pilot' document provides detailed specifications for materials, connectors, and anchoring
solutions that can accommodate a range of Sea State Codes (SSCs). The specifications ensure that the FPV systems
are equipped with appropriate materials and anchoring methods, enhancing their durability and performance in diverse
environmental conditions.

                                                                            To reduce these risks, we will :

                                                                            progressing to system-level integration in controlled conditions, will aid in the early
                                                                            identification and mitigation of risks during the development process.

REVISION LOG ­ HISTORY OF CHANGES

March 20th
    · Included comments on references for cost reductions in Section 2.1.1, page 19: 2) A 10% reduction in
         LCOE for the new CTI technology, compared to a reference LCOE for partner 2/3 calculated based on
         a project case in France with CTI's Hydrelio (standard) technology, 3) A reduction of 15% in BoS costs
         for Zimmermann's FPV technology, compared to ZIM BoS cost in 2023, 4) A reduction in LCOE of
         60% for Sunlit's FPV technology, primarily based on cost reductions of the aluminum floater compared
         to production costs as of March 2023, due to more mature processing with reduced labor and material
         costs.
    · Included a sentence answering review shortcoming: "The new technology used for the 5 MW pilot
         consists of radically new and lighter floaters which reduce transportation costs significantly, and the new
         O&M concept, where O&M is carried out without operators stepping on the modules." in Task 4.1 on
         page 37.
    · Sentence changed: "A pre-pilot of 50kW will be first installed to assess the new system design and
         validate its deployment for the 5MW pilot" is changed to "A pilot of 50 kW will be installed to assess
         the new system design for a broader range of wave conditions" in Task 4.1 on page 37.
    · Included sentence related to common information and dissemination activities upon invitation by the
         CINEA in the description of WP7 on page 40.
    · Added text in WP6, Task 6.2, page 40: "Enhancing the hinge strength is critical for the durability and
         reliability of Sunlit Sea's FPV systems. This is thoroughly addressed in SuRE, in collaboration with
         SABIC. The other central development to withstand harsher conditions, is and a shift from butyl/silicon
         encapsulants to polyolefin elastomer (POE). This shift will leverage MetSolar's cutting-edge capabilities
         in lamination techniques by transitioning from the current potting process to advanced lamination
         techniques. MetSolar's extensive experience in metal lamination will guide our exploration of using
         polyolefin elastomer (POE) instead of traditional butyl/silicon encapsulants."

Call: [HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01-03] -- [Floating PV Systems]

                                                                 EU Grants: Application form (HE RIA and IA): V3.2 ­ 15.11.2022
                                                            Associated with document Ref. Ares(2024)4725898 - 01/07/2024

1  Excellence

Scaling up renewable energy (RE): rapidly and sustainably. Solar photovoltaic (PV) technology is widely
recognized as key to realizing fully decarbonized energy generation and a main lever to achieve the European Green
Deal objectives while phasing out our dependence on Russian fossil fuels. PV module installation rates have
increased tremendously over the last ten years, driven by reduced costs of PV and increased climate awareness. We
need to keep increasing the deployment rate for many years to come if we are to meet EU policy goals1. With
increasing PV deployment, issues related to land access will become increasingly important. Integrating PV in
architecture, infrastructure and agriculture are promising solutions to abate land scarcity. Floating PV (FPV) is
another2.

                                                                                    Through SuRE we will enhance the
                                                                                    affordability and sustainability of FPV.
                                                                                    Enhanced Sustainability will be achieved by
                                                                                    reducing the carbon footprints and enhance
                                                                                    positive synergies with the local environment.
                                                                                    The inherent modularity of PV provides an
                                                                                    opportunity to tailor coverage ratios, light
                                                                                    penetration, and layout to achieve FPV power
                                                                                    plants with a positive impact on the local
                                                                                    environment and biodiversity. We will
                                                                                    achieve enhanced affordability by making
                                                                                    more Reliable and cost-Efficient FPV power
                                                                                    plants, developing models and framework that
                                                                                    reduce the financial and reliability-related
                                                                                    risks of this still young technology. Finally,
                                                                                    we use existing and new knowledge to
significantly improve the designs of three specific FPV technologies. These include the two most widely used FPV
technologies in Europe: Ciel et Terre (CTI) and Zimmermann (ZIM). The third innovative FPV technology, Sunlit
Sea (Sunlit), aims at offshore applications. All three technologies have separate and concrete routes laid out in SuRE
to reduce LCOE through enhanced reliability and cost-efficiency, while reducing carbon footprints and enabling a
positive impact on the local biodiversity. The activities to reach these goals are structured by entwining 3
generalizable work packages (WPs), WP1 Sustainability, WP2 Reliability, and WP3 Efficiency, with 3 value-chain
specific WPs focused on innovations for the 3 FPV technologies, WP4 CTI, WP5 ZIM, and WP6 Sunlit, as illustrated
in Fig. 1. This approach benefits both a broad European FPV industry and the individual FPV value chains in SuRE.
Consequently, SuRE provides a clear pathway to substantially increase the deployment of FPV in Europe and expand
the application of FPV on inland and offshore waters, in line with the EU strategic priorities in Table 1.1.

1.1 Objectives and ambition #@PRJ-OBJ-PO@#
1.1.1 Objectives and relation to the work programme

SuRE provides targeted answers and measurable solutions to the topic HORIZON-CL5-2023-D3-01-03: Floating
PV Systems, addressing systematically the five objectives of the Scope. The consortium has set a robust
implementation plan to ensure the success of SuRE and de-risk the project.

The overall SuRE objective is to increase competitiveness of FPV power plants compared to fossil fuels
   and land-consuming renewable technologies by enabling sustainable design, reducing LCOE and
            expanding the application potential for three leading European FPV technologies.

This objective of SuRE is based on long-term development plans of the three FPV technology providers in the

consortium: 1) Ciel et Terre (CTI, France) was early to provide utility-scale FPV solutions. Since 2011, they have

installed 650 MW globally, with 1.2 GW under development. Their subsidiary Laketricity (LKY, France), devoted

to utility-scale FPV projects development, has delivered 90 projects in 10 countries. CTI and LKY will build a 5 MW

FPV pilot plant situated on Lac de Tertre, Saint Ghislain in Belgium, using CTIs recently prototyped floater design.

This new design is a radical departure from their existing Hydrelio technology. It provides reduced costs, increased

sustainability, and expanded waterbodies for installation. 2) Zimmermann PV-Steel Group (ZIM, Germany) and

their long-term developer partner, BayWa r.e., (BayWa, Germany) have installed 210 MW of FPV, including the

Sellingen project in the Netherlands which is the largest FPV system outside of Asia. ZIM and BayWa will build a 5

MW FPV pilot in the Netherlands, to extend their FPV design for more waterbodies while advancing on all SuRE

fronts. BayWa has a pipeline of more than 2 GW of FPV projects. 3) Sunlit Sea (Sunlit, Norway) has developed a

floater-module integrated FPV system. They have several pilot projects in Norwegian and US waters. Metsolar

(Lithuania) makes custom PV modules and has worked with Sunlit for years. Sunlit will update their design to

decrease resource burden and production cost by using SuRE to bring new technologies into their production line.

During the project, SuRE will build a 105 kW pilot at Singløya, Norway. Augmenting these 3 independent FPV value

chains, SuRE includes SABIC (Netherlands) as a technical innovator of bio- and circular plastics for the FPV market.

SABIC will work across the value chains and with the four independent research institutes to deliver plastic

sustainably. Four research institutes and one science communication association complete the SuRE consortium: IFE

(Norway) coordinates SuRE, Fraunhofer (Germany) is the largest solar research institute in Europe, TNO

(Netherlands) is an independent research association for targeted innovation, Deltares (Netherlands) is an
